Researchers wanted to share expensive computers and build communication that could keep working when parts failed. ARPANET grew through the 1970s. On 1 January 1983, it adopted TCP/IP. Academic networks expanded during the 1980s, and commercial access spread in the 1990s as personal computers and the web became popular.

Why it mattered scientifically

The internet showed that a decentralised network could grow to a global scale. Its design expects links and machines to fail, so packets can often use another route. This approach supported new fields such as network science and cybersecurity. Open standards allowed equipment from different makers to communicate.

How it shaped later developments

Email, file transfer, the web, streaming, online games, and cloud services all run over the internet. The domain name system gives readable names to numerical addresses. Encryption protects many connections. Mobile and satellite networks now provide more ways to reach the same global network.

How it changed human life

People can publish, learn, trade, and organise across borders almost instantly. Businesses and public services moved online. The same network also carries fraud, harassment, and false information, while unequal access leaves some communities behind. The internet is shared infrastructure, not one company or one website.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Internet Society. 'A Brief History of the Internet.' https://www.internetsociety.org/internet/history-internet/brief-history-internet/
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Internet.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/Internet

D. Museum and archive resources

  1. Computer History Museum. 'Internet History Program.' https://www.computerhistory.org/internethistory/

6.11Laser

Key figure(s): Theodore Maiman; Charles Townes; Arthur Schawlow

A laser makes a narrow, orderly beam of light that can be focused very precisely. Lasers now read barcodes, carry internet traffic, cut metal, and perform surgery.

Period: 1960 (first working laser, Theodore Maiman); 1961 (early medical use) | Key figure(s): Theodore Maiman; Charles Townes; Arthur Schawlow

Key facts

  • The maser (microwave) came first; Townes, Basov, and Prokhorov shared a Nobel Prize
  • Theodore Maiman demonstrated the first optical laser in 1960
  • Laser means Light Amplification by Stimulated Emission of Radiation
  • Coherent light can be focused to a tiny, intense spot
  • Uses range from welding and surgery to fibre-optic communications

What it is

A laser produces light whose waves stay in step, a property called coherence. Its beam is usually narrow and has a small range of colours. Inside the device, energy excites atoms or other material. One photon can then trigger the release of a matching photon, a process called stimulated emission. It is like a crowd clapping in time instead of making random noise.

Who discovered or invented it

Albert Einstein described stimulated emission in 1917. Charles Townes and colleagues built a maser, which works with microwaves, in the 1950s. Alexander Prokhorov and Nikolay Basov did related work, while Arthur Schawlow and Townes described an optical version. Theodore Maiman built the first working laser with a ruby crystal in 1960.

When and how it emerged

The invention grew from quantum physics, better crystals, and research funding during the 1950s. Several teams raced to make a working device. Maiman succeeded by using a bright flash lamp to excite a ruby rod. Gas, semiconductor, and fibre lasers followed as researchers learned to match different materials to different tasks.

Why it mattered scientifically

Lasers gave scientists an unusually precise source of light. They improved the measurement of distance, time, motion, and chemical composition. Holography and gravitational-wave detectors also depend on coherent light. The laser was both proof that quantum ideas could make a practical device and a new tool for testing nature.

How it shaped later developments

Small diode lasers made CD and DVD players, barcode scanners, printers, and fibre-optic communication practical. Powerful lasers cut and weld materials. Medical lasers reshape corneas and treat some skin and blood-vessel problems. Research systems use lasers to cool atoms and to study nuclear fusion.

How it changed human life

A supermarket scanner and an eye surgeon use the same basic idea: put controlled light exactly where it is needed. Lasers made manufacturing cuts cleaner and communication faster. They also created safety risks because a strong beam can damage an eye before a person can react. Rules, warning labels, and protective equipment became essential.

Sources and references

B. Original scientific papers

  1. Maiman, T. H. 'Stimulated Optical Radiation in Ruby.' Nature 187 (1960): 493-494. https://doi.org/10.1038/187493a0

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Laser.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/laser
  2. American Physical Society. 'May 16, 1960: Maiman Demonstrates the First Working Laser.' https://www.aps.org/apsnews/2010/05/first-working-laser

G. Scientific organisations

  1. Nobel Prize. 'The Nobel Prize in Physics 1964.' https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/1964/summary/

6.12Photography

Key figure(s): Joseph Nicéphore Niépce; Louis Daguerre; William Henry Fox Talbot; later chemists, camera makers, and sensor engineers

Photography uses light to form and preserve an image on a lasting surface. It turned passing scenes into evidence, art, news, and family memory, and later filled digital devices with instant pictures.

Period: Early permanent images in the 1820s; major public announcements in 1839; film and digital developments followed | Key figure(s): Joseph Nicéphore Niépce; Louis Daguerre; William Henry Fox Talbot; later chemists, camera makers, and sensor engineers

Key facts

  • Joseph Nicéphore Niépce made early lasting camera images before 1830
  • Louis Daguerre's daguerreotype process was announced in 1839
  • William Henry Fox Talbot developed paper negative-positive methods that allowed multiple prints
  • Faster light-sensitive materials and shutters made candid and motion photography possible
  • Digital sensors have replaced film in most everyday cameras

What it is

Photography forms an image with light and stores it on metal, glass, film, paper, or a digital sensor. A lens focuses light from a scene. In film photography, light changes sensitive chemicals; in a digital camera, a sensor turns light into electrical signals. A camera works somewhat like an eye, but it can preserve what it receives. The result records a real pattern of light, though framing, timing, staging, and editing still shape its meaning.

Who discovered or invented it

Photography had several inventors. Joseph Nicéphore Niépce made the earliest surviving photograph from a camera in the 1820s. Louis Daguerre worked with him and later developed the daguerreotype, a sharp image on a silver-coated plate, announced in 1839. William Henry Fox Talbot developed paper negatives and positive prints. Unlike a daguerreotype, one negative could produce many copies. Chemists, lens makers, studio workers, and manufacturers then made photography faster, cheaper, and easier.

When and how it emerged

Experiments joined two older ideas: the camera obscura, which projects an image, and chemicals that change in light. After the announcements of 1839, portrait studios opened rapidly, though early sitters had to remain still for long exposures. Wet plates and then dry plates shortened preparation and exposure times. Roll film and simple cameras brought photography to amateurs in the late nineteenth century. Colour film, small cameras, electronic sensors, and smartphones later made pictures immediate and easy to copy.

Why it mattered scientifically

Photography joined optics with photochemistry, the study of chemical changes caused by light. It gave scientists a record that could outlast the moment and be measured later. Astronomers collected faint light over long exposures. Biologists recorded microscope views, and motion studies broke a galloping horse or a flying bird into separate instants. Digital sensors continue the same task with semiconductors. The camera became a second pair of eyes, able to gather evidence too faint, fast, small, or distant for ordinary sight.

How it shaped later developments

Cinema grew from sequences of photographs shown quickly. X-ray imaging, aerial surveys, satellite cameras, and scientific detectors all preserve patterns of radiation. Newspapers, advertising, fashion, and identification systems came to depend on images. Digital photography led to camera phones, instant global sharing, and huge image collections used by computer vision. Editing also became easier, increasing the need to check a picture's source and context.

How it changed human life

Families could keep the faces of distant relatives and people who had died. News photographs brought wars, disasters, celebrations, and protests to a wide public. Pictures supported medicine, mapping, police work, and identity documents. They also enabled surveillance and weakened privacy as cameras became common. A photograph can feel like a window onto a moment, but it never shows everything outside its frame. That mix of evidence, choice, and emotion still gives photography its power.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Newhall, Beaumont. The History of Photography. 5th ed. New York: Museum of Modern Art, 1982.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'History of photography.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/photography
  2. The Metropolitan Museum of Art. 'Daguerre (1787-1851) and the Invention of Photography.' https://www.metmuseum.org/toah/hd/dagu/hd_dagu.htm

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Smithsonian Magazine. 'This Is How Photography Was First Invented.' https://www.smithsonianmag.com/innovation/this-is-how-photography-was-first-invented-180972188/

6.13Public-Key Cryptography

Key figure(s): Whitfield Diffie; Martin Hellman; Ron Rivest; Adi Shamir; Leonard Adleman; earlier GCHQ researchers (classified)

Public-key cryptography lets strangers share secrets and prove identity without first meeting to swap a private key. It underpins secure web shopping, software updates, and much of internet trust.

Period: c. 1976 (Diffie–Hellman); 1977–1978 (RSA); earlier classified work later revealed | Key figure(s): Whitfield Diffie; Martin Hellman; Ron Rivest; Adi Shamir; Leonard Adleman; earlier GCHQ researchers (classified)

Key facts

  • Diffie and Hellman published public-key agreement ideas in 1976
  • RSA (1977–1978) offered a practical encryption and signature scheme based on factoring
  • Each user can publish a public key while keeping a matching private key secret
  • HTTPS and many messaging apps rely on public-key building blocks
  • Related ideas were developed earlier in secret government research, revealed later

What it is

Public-key cryptography uses linked key pairs: a public key anyone may see and a private key only one party holds. You can encrypt a message to someone's public key so only their private key opens it, or verify a signature made with a private key. Key-agreement protocols let two parties derive a shared secret over an open channel. Think of padlocks you can copy and give away for sealing boxes, while only one key still opens them.

Who discovered or invented it

Whitfield Diffie and Martin Hellman openly described public-key distribution in 1976. Rivest, Shamir, and Adleman published RSA soon after. British GCHQ researchers had earlier classified work on similar ideas, acknowledged years later. Academic and industry cryptographers then built protocols, certificates, and standards. The breakthrough is conceptual as much as algorithmic.

When and how it emerged

After the mid-1970s papers, adoption waited on faster computers, careful implementations, and certificate authorities. The 1990s web boom made SSL/TLS and HTTPS practical necessities for commerce. Mobile messaging and software signing followed. Threats from future quantum computers now drive 'post-quantum' designs — the same need for trust between strangers, with new maths.

Why it mattered scientifically

Public-key crypto rests on hard mathematical problems — factoring, discrete logarithms, elliptic curves — linking number theory to engineering. Security proofs and attack models became a discipline. It showed that secrecy can be negotiated in public view. Computer science gained a foundation for authentication at internet scale. Without it, a secure channel between strangers stays a metaphor.

How it shaped later developments

Online banking, e-commerce, and app stores depend on certificates and signatures. VPNs, cryptocurrencies, and secure email build on the same primitives. Key-management failures teach operational security. Laws and export controls fought over the right to strong crypto. The quiet padlock in a browser is public-key engineering made mundane.

How it changed human life

People buy, bank, and speak across continents with less fear of casual eavesdropping — though not with perfect safety. Trust shifted partly from personal meetings to mathematical assurance and institutions that vouch for keys. Scams evolved too. Still, modern networked life would stall if every private chat first needed a couriered secret codebook.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Singh, Simon. The Code Book: The Science of Secrecy from Ancient Egypt to Quantum Cryptography. New York: Doubleday, 1999.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Public-key cryptography.' https://www.britannica.com/topic/public-key-cryptography
  2. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy. 'Cryptography.' https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/cryptography/

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Computer History Museum. Cryptography and public-key milestones. https://www.computerhistory.org/

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Scientific American. 'How Public Key Cryptography Works.' https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/how-public-key-cryptography-works/

6.14Radio

Key figure(s): Guglielmo Marconi; Nikola Tesla; Heinrich Hertz

Radio carries speech, music, or data on electromagnetic waves through the air. It first supported wireless messages and later let one broadcaster reach millions of listeners at once.

Period: 1895-1901 (Marconi's wireless telegraphy); broadcasting from 1920s | Key figure(s): Guglielmo Marconi; Nikola Tesla; Heinrich Hertz

Key facts

  • Maxwell predicted, and Hertz produced, radio waves in the laboratory
  • Guglielmo Marconi built practical wireless telegraphy in the 1890s
  • Many others, including Tesla, Lodge, and Popov, contributed key pieces
  • Voice broadcasting blossomed after World War I
  • Radio made news and entertainment a shared live event

What it is

Radio uses electromagnetic waves, which are moving electric and magnetic fields, to carry information without a connecting wire. A transmitter places speech, music, or data onto a carrier wave. A receiver tuned to the right frequency recovers that information. Tuning is like choosing one conversation in a crowded room.

Who discovered or invented it

James Clerk Maxwell predicted electromagnetic waves, and Heinrich Hertz produced and detected them in the laboratory. In the 1890s, Guglielmo Marconi built a successful wireless telegraph business. Nikola Tesla, Oliver Lodge, Alexander Popov, Karl Ferdinand Braun, and others developed important circuits, antennas, and detectors. Radio therefore has many contributors rather than one clear inventor.

When and how it emerged

Early radio carried Morse code between ships and shore stations. The Titanic disaster in 1912 showed both its life-saving value and the need for rules. Vacuum tubes later made speech and music transmission stronger and clearer. Regular broadcasting spread rapidly during the 1920s, and governments began licensing stations and frequencies.

Why it mattered scientifically

Radio turned Maxwell's theory into a practical technology used every day. It led scientists to study the ionosphere, which can reflect some radio waves, and bandwidth, the amount of information a channel can carry. Because many users share the spectrum, engineering and regulation must prevent signals from interfering with one another.

How it shaped later developments

Radar, television, mobile phones, satellite links, Bluetooth, and Wi-Fi all use radio waves. They operate at different frequencies and encode information in different ways. FM improved sound quality for music, while digital systems fit more data into a channel. Emergency services still rely on radio when other networks fail.

How it changed human life

A single live voice could enter millions of homes, like one speaker addressing an audience spread across a country. Radio brought news, sport, music, and lessons to cities and isolated farms. It also became a tool for propaganda. It helped create national celebrities and a shared sense of hearing events as they happened.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Radio technology.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/radio-technology
  2. BBC. 'History of the BBC.' https://www.bbc.com/historyofthebbc

G. Scientific organisations

  1. Nobel Prize. 'The Nobel Prize in Physics 1909.' https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/1909/summary/

H. Professional societies

  1. Engineering and Technology History Wiki. 'Radio.' https://ethw.org/Radio

6.15Recorded Sound

Key figure(s): Thomas Edison; Emile Berliner; Alexander Graham Bell's Volta Laboratory; later tape and digital audio engineers

Recorded sound stores voice and music as physical or digital traces that can be played back later. From cylinders and discs to tape and files, it made performance portable and repeatable.

Period: c. 1877 (Edison phonograph); 1880s–1890s disc and cylinder commerce; later magnetic tape and digital audio | Key figure(s): Thomas Edison; Emile Berliner; Alexander Graham Bell's Volta Laboratory; later tape and digital audio engineers

Key facts

  • Thomas Edison demonstrated a working phonograph in 1877 that could record and replay sound
  • Emile Berliner popularised flat disc records and the gramophone approach in the 1880s–1890s
  • Magnetic tape later made editing and longer takes practical for studios and radio
  • Electrical recording and amplification raised fidelity far above early acoustic horns
  • Digital audio formats turned sound into copyable data while keeping the same core idea: capture, store, replay

What it is

Recorded sound is any system that writes acoustic vibrations into a lasting medium and later turns those traces back into audible waves. Early machines cut grooves in foil, wax, or shellac; later systems magnetised tape or sampled air pressure as numbers. Playback reconstitutes the pattern through a needle, head, or digital converter and a speaker. In everyday language, it is a performance you can hear again without the performers in the room.

Who discovered or invented it

Thomas Edison is credited with the first practical phonograph that both recorded and reproduced sound in 1877. Alexander Graham Bell's Volta Laboratory improved wax cylinders, and Emile Berliner shifted commerce toward stamped flat discs. Studio engineers, pressing plants, and later tape and digital designers extended the craft. Famous names mark milestones; the industry is a long chain of makers, musicians, and listeners.

When and how it emerged

After Edison's 1877 demonstration, parlour machines and coin-operated phonographs spread in the 1880s–1890s. Disc records and mass pressing made hits reproducible at industrial scale. Electrical recording in the 1920s, magnetic tape mid-century, and compact discs then files each rewrote the medium without abandoning capture-and-replay. Radio and cinema absorbed the same logic. The calendar is a stack of formats, not one birthday.

Why it mattered scientifically

Recording forced precise thinking about vibration, frequency, and conversion from air to solid and back. It created archives for linguistics, ethnomusicology, and acoustics research. Signal processing, noise reduction, and sampling theory grew partly to serve music and speech capture. Scientifically, recorded sound turned ephemeral waves into data that could be measured, compared, and edited.

How it shaped later developments

The recording industry remade music careers and copyright. Sound cinema, radio libraries, podcasts, and streaming all assume stored audio. Telephony, voice mail, and voice assistants reuse capture-and-replay. Standards for discs, tape speeds, and file formats became global infrastructure.

How it changed human life

Families heard absent voices; nations shared anthems and hit songs on demand. Music listening shifted from live rooms to private headphones and public broadcasts. New jobs appeared in studios, pressing plants, and retail, while touring economies reorganised around recorded fame. Privacy and piracy debates followed every format change.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Millard, Andre. America on Record: A History of Recorded Sound. 2nd ed. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 2005.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Phonograph.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/phonograph
  2. Library of Congress. History of Edison sound recordings. https://www.loc.gov/collections/edison-company-motion-pictures-and-sound-recordings/articles-and-essays/history-of-edison-sound-recordings/

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Sound recording objects and stories. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories
  2. Library of Congress. National Jukebox — early recorded sound. https://www.loc.gov/collections/national-jukebox/

6.16Satellite Technology

Key figure(s): Soviet space programme; NASA; Arthur C. Clarke

An artificial satellite is a machine placed in orbit to observe, communicate, navigate, or study. Since Sputnik 1 in 1957, satellites have become essential to weather forecasts, television, maps, and science.

Period: 1957 (Sputnik 1); 1963 (Syncom 2 reached geosynchronous orbit); 1964 (Syncom 3, first geostationary communications satellite) | Key figure(s): Soviet space programme; NASA; Arthur C. Clarke

Key facts

  • Sputnik 1, launched by the Soviet Union on 4 October 1957, was the first artificial satellite
  • Arthur C. Clarke popularised the idea of geostationary communications orbits
  • Weather, navigation, science, and spy satellites followed
  • Orbit is a free-fall path fast enough to keep missing Earth
  • Space debris is a growing management problem

What it is

An artificial satellite is a machine that travels around Earth or another body. Orbit is continuous free fall: the satellite moves sideways so quickly that it keeps missing the ground. Some satellites observe clouds or land. Others relay calls, television, and data. A rocket delivers the satellite, but the satellite carries out the long-term job.

Who discovered or invented it

The Soviet Union launched Sputnik 1, the first artificial satellite, in 1957. Sergei Korolev led its rocket programme. The United States launched Explorer 1 in 1958, with teams involving the Jet Propulsion Laboratory and Wernher von Braun's group. Arthur C. Clarke had described communication relays in geostationary orbit in 1945.

When and how it emerged

Rocket development during and after the Second World War made orbital launches possible. The Cold War turned satellites into a contest over military and scientific power. Sputnik's radio beeps shocked the public and accelerated the space race. Weather, communication, navigation, and reconnaissance satellites soon followed.

Why it mattered scientifically

Satellites let researchers observe the whole Earth repeatedly. They measure weather, sea level, ice, gravity, and changes in forests and cities. Remote sensing means collecting information from a distance, often with cameras or radar. The result is a moving record of the planet rather than observations from only scattered ground stations.

How it shaped later developments

Satellites enabled GPS, live international television, global weather models, disaster mapping, and some internet connections. Small satellites can now work in large groups called constellations. These groups increase coverage but also add crowding, radio interference, and collision risk. Space debris can remain in orbit and threaten later missions.

How it changed human life

People check satellite-based forecasts before travelling and watch events broadcast across oceans. Farmers monitor crops, rescuers map floods, and scientists track climate change. A weather satellite is like a camera on a very high balcony, returning to view huge areas again and again. Military surveillance and civilian convenience grew from the same basic technology.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Artificial satellite.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/artificial-satellite
  2. Smithsonian National Air and Space Museum. 'Sputnik and the Dawn of the Space Age.' https://airandspace.si.edu/stories/editorial/sputnik-and-dawn-space-age

G. Space organisations

  1. NASA. 'What Is a Satellite?' https://www.nasa.gov/general/what-is-a-satellite/
  2. European Space Agency. 'Space debris by the numbers.' https://www.esa.int/Space_Safety/Space_Debris/Space_debris_by_the_numbers

6.17Search Engines

Key figure(s): Alan Emtage (Archie, 1990); Larry Page and Sergey Brin (Google, 1998)

A search engine finds and ranks web pages that match a query. It turned an unreadable flood of sites into something you can ask in a sentence.

Period: 1990 (Archie); mid-1990s crawler engines; 1998 (Google founded) | Key figure(s): Alan Emtage (Archie, 1990); Larry Page and Sergey Brin (Google, 1998)

Key facts

  • Early tools included Archie (1990) and later crawler indexes such as AltaVista
  • Larry Page and Sergey Brin developed PageRank at Stanford in the late 1990s
  • Google launched in 1998 and made relevance a mass-market product
  • A crawler is a program that follows links and copies page text
  • Ranking, not only storing, is what makes search useful

What it is

A search engine collects information about web pages, stores an index — a giant lookup table — and returns a ranked list when you type a query. A crawler, sometimes called a spider, is the program that follows links to discover pages. Ranking tries to put the most useful result first. Without search, the web is a city without street signs.

Who discovered or invented it

Alan Emtage's Archie (1990) searched file listings on early internet hosts. Hand-made directories such as early Yahoo listed sites by category. Crawler-based engines such as AltaVista showed that the whole web could be indexed. At Stanford, Larry Page and Sergey Brin treated links as votes of importance (PageRank) and founded Google in 1998. Many others built competing indexes. The breakthrough was quality of ranking plus speed, not the first crawler alone.

When and how it emerged

By the mid-1990s the web was too big to browse page by page. Universities had both the machines and the problem. Cheap commodity computers and a flood of amateur pages made automatic indexing necessary. Advertising later paid for free search for users. The late 1990s created a new kind of company: one that organises other people's information.

Why it mattered scientifically

Search is applied information retrieval, linguistics, and later machine learning. It raised hard questions: what counts as relevance? how do you fight spam? how do you treat languages and names fairly? Scientifically it made the web itself an object of measurement. The index is a changing map of human expression.

How it shaped later developments

Specialised search, maps, scholar indexes, and AI-generated answers are sequels. Search shaped what is profitable to publish through search-engine optimisation. Results can influence elections, health choices, and reputations. The address bar and the search box taught people to ask the web as if it were an oracle.

How it changed human life

Daily life gained instant lookup of facts, places, and how-to knowledge. Homework, medical self-checks, and small-business survival changed. People also outsourced memory and met filter bubbles. Society now argues about ranking as it once argued about library catalogues. Finding is a form of power, and search engines hold a great deal of it.

Sources and references

B. Peer-reviewed articles and original scientific papers

  1. Brin, Sergey, and Lawrence Page. 'The Anatomy of a Large-Scale Hypertextual Web Search Engine.' Stanford Infolab technical report / WWW7, 1998. http://infolab.stanford.edu/~backrub/google.html

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Search engine.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/search-engine
  2. Google. 'How Search works.' https://www.google.com/search/howsearchworks/

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Computer History Museum. 'Internet History — Search Engines.' https://www.computerhistory.org/internethistory/1990s/

6.18Semiconductor Technology

Key figure(s): Bell Labs; Texas Instruments; Intel; TSMC; ASML

Semiconductor technology is the craft of making and controlling materials that sit between metals and insulators. It is the industrial base of chips, solar cells, and LED light.

Period: 1947 (transistor); 1958 (IC); 1960s–present (Moore's Law scaling) | Key figure(s): Bell Labs; Texas Instruments; Intel; TSMC; ASML

Key facts

  • Silicon is the dominant semiconductor for computer chips
  • Doping adds tiny amounts of other elements to tune conduction
  • Fabrication uses ultra-clean rooms and photolithography
  • Moore's law described decades of shrinking features
  • The industry is now a strategic pillar of the world economy

What it is

A semiconductor is a material whose electrical conduction can be adjusted with heat, light, or tiny added impurities (doping). Silicon, germanium, and compounds such as gallium arsenide are examples. Semiconductor technology is the whole chain: growing crystals, slicing wafers, printing patterns, and packaging chips. It is not one device. It is a manufacturing civilisation.

Who discovered or invented it

Nineteenth-century physicists noticed odd conduction in some crystals. The transistor (1947) made the field urgent. Shockley's lab, Fairchild, Intel, TSMC, and many others turned physics into a process. No single inventor owns 'semiconductors'. The heroes are often process engineers whose names never enter textbooks.

When and how it emerged

Post-war electronics, then the space race and consumer gadgets, paid for cleaner and finer fabrication. The circumstances included chemical engineering, optics, and enormous capital. By the late twentieth century a leading factory cost billions and had to stay at the frontier or die. Geography clustered: Silicon Valley, later East Asia.

Why it mattered scientifically

The field is applied quantum mechanics and materials chemistry. Band theory explains why silicon can switch. Defects, once enemies, are sometimes tools. Scientifically it showed that atomic-scale control can be industrial, not only experimental. Yield — how many chips work — became a science of its own.

How it shaped later developments

Solar photovoltaics, LEDs, power electronics for electric cars, and sensors all grew from the same materials family. Extreme ultraviolet lithography is a recent leap for smaller transistors. Research into new materials (beyond silicon) continues. Chip export rules now shape diplomacy. The technology's sequels are economic as much as technical.

How it changed human life

Almost every modern convenience silently assumes semiconductor plants exist. Light bulbs became LEDs; cameras became sensors; cars became computers. Societies that cannot make or buy chips cannot fully join digital life. Human progress is now tied to clean rooms most people will never see. That hidden dependence is the story of this technology.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Miller, Chris. Chip War. New York: Scribner, 2022.
  2. Winchester, Simon. The Perfectionists. New York: HarperCollins, 2018.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. ASML. Lithography technology overview. https://www.asml.com/en/technology
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Semiconductor.' https://www.britannica.com/science/semiconductor

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Computer History Museum. Semiconductor manufacturing history. https://www.computerhistory.org/siliconengine/

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Council on Foreign Relations. Conversation with Chris Miller on chip geopolitics. https://www.cfr.org/articles/war-over-worlds-most-critical-technology-conversation-chris-miller

6.19Smartphones

Key figure(s): IBM (Simon, 1994); Apple (iPhone, 2007, Steve Jobs); Google (Android, 2008)

A smartphone is a pocket computer that makes calls, goes online, and uses a touchscreen to run apps. After 2007 it became many people's main door to digital life.

Period: 1992–1994 (IBM Simon, early smartphone concept); 2007 (iPhone, mass-market shift) | Key figure(s): IBM (Simon, 1994); Apple (iPhone, 2007, Steve Jobs); Google (Android, 2008)

Key facts

  • Earlier phones and PDAs (such as BlackBerry and Nokia communicators) pointed the way
  • Apple's iPhone (2007) combined a real mobile web browser, multi-touch screen, and later an app store
  • Google's Android opened a similar model to many manufacturers
  • The device merges phone, camera, GPS, and computer
  • For many people it is their first — and only — computer

What it is

A smartphone is a mobile phone built around a general-purpose computer, a high-resolution touchscreen, and a frequent internet link. Apps are small programs for maps, payments, messages, or news. Sensors such as GPS, a motion sensor, and a camera let the device know where it is and what it sees. Think of it as a Swiss Army knife that also rings: one slab replaces the separate phone, camera, map, and wallet for many daily jobs.

Who discovered or invented it

IBM's Simon (1994), Nokia, BlackBerry, and others made early smart-ish phones. Apple, under Steve Jobs, launched the iPhone in 2007 with multi-touch and a real mobile browser; the App Store (2008) created a software marketplace. Google's Android, with many hardware partners, spread the model worldwide. Credit also belongs to touchscreen researchers, ARM chip designers, and mobile-network engineers. The product is a stack of inventions.

When and how it emerged

By the mid-2000s, networks were fast enough and chips were small enough. Wi-Fi, 3G, lithium-ion batteries, and a web full of services made the leap possible. Within a decade, billions of people who had never owned a PC were online. In much of the world the smartphone leapfrogged the desktop.

Why it mattered scientifically

The device is a triumph of packing: radio, optics, power management, and human-interface design in one package. Mobile computing became the main form of computing for many users. Location and attention turned into scientific and commercial data. Battery chemistry and network delay suddenly mattered to everyone. The lab moved into the hand.

How it shaped later developments

Tablets, wearables, and whole app economies followed. Mobile payments, social media, and turn-by-turn navigation became default habits. Repairability, short product lives, and mineral supply chains are the shadow side. Foldable screens and on-device AI are later skins on the same idea. The phone absorbed the camera, the map, the wallet, and the newspaper.

How it changed human life

People gained access to knowledge, banking, and distant family — and a device that can interrupt every silence. Work followed employees home. A good phone can be a ladder out of isolation; addiction and surveillance can be a trap. Society is still inventing manners for a machine that is always there. Life became more connected and more distracted in the same gesture.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Isaacson, Walter. Steve Jobs. New York: Simon & Schuster, 2011.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Smartphone.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/smartphone
  2. Pew Research Center. Mobile fact sheet. https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/fact-sheet/mobile/

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Computer History Museum. Mobile computing collections and exhibits. https://computerhistory.org/

H. Professional societies and standards bodies

  1. IEEE Spectrum. Histories of the iPhone and mobile platforms. https://spectrum.ieee.org/

6.20SMS and Instant Messaging

Key figure(s): GSM signalling engineers (e.g. Friedhelm Hillebrand and colleagues); early IM services; later mobile chat platforms

SMS and instant messaging send short text between people in near real time on phones and computers. They made quick written talk ordinary — from 160-character texts to always-on chat threads.

Period: c. 1992–1993 first SMS; 1990s–2000s IM clients; later smartphone chat apps on data networks | Key figure(s): GSM signalling engineers (e.g. Friedhelm Hillebrand and colleagues); early IM services; later mobile chat platforms

Key facts

  • SMS was defined in GSM mobile standards and saw early public messages around 1992–1993
  • Classic SMS messages were short (about 160 characters in Latin alphabets) and used signalling channels
  • Desktop instant messengers in the 1990s added presence, buddy lists, and rapid back-and-forth
  • Smartphone apps later carried chat, images, and voice notes over internet data
  • Texting culture reshaped etiquette, activism, and customer alerts worldwide

What it is

SMS is the Short Message Service that carries brief texts across mobile networks, originally through spare signalling capacity. Instant messaging is a broader family of apps that show who is online and exchange lines in a live thread, often over the internet. Both prioritise speed and brevity over formal letters. In everyday language, they are typed talk that arrives in seconds.

Who discovered or invented it

GSM committees and engineers such as Friedhelm Hillebrand helped specify SMS as a network feature. Operators and handset makers implemented send and receive. Separate software firms built ICQ, AIM, and other IM clients for PCs. Later platforms merged SMS habits with data chat. Standards and products share the credit.

When and how it emerged

After early 1990s SMS demos, prepaid phones and teen culture exploded texting in the late 1990s–2000s. IM rode office LANs and dial-up at the same time. Smartphones folded both into notification-centric apps; over-the-top messengers competed with carrier SMS. Emoji, read receipts, and group chats thickened the medium. The thread replaced many phone calls for logistics among friends.

Why it mattered scientifically

SMS stressed reliable store-and-forward on constrained radio channels and roaming agreements. IM research studied presence, concurrency, and social-graph design. Linguistics tracked abbreviation and emoji as data. Scientifically, short messaging is a natural experiment in compressed human communication.

How it shaped later developments

Two-factor codes, appointment reminders, and disaster alerts ride SMS reliability. Chatbots and customer support copy IM interfaces. Push notifications inherit the interruption model of a new text. Social networks borrowed the always-available thread.

How it changed human life

Plans coordinated without voice calls; diaspora families kept cheap contact; workplaces gained quick pings and after-hours creep. Misunderstandings thrived without tone; scams arrived as texts. Attention fractured into badge counts. Yet for a generation, 'just text me' became the default social glue.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Hillebrand, Friedhelm, ed. Short Message Service (SMS): The Creation of Personal Global Text Messaging. Chichester: Wiley, 2010.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Text messaging.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/text-messaging

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Computer History Museum. Mobile communications and messaging resources. https://www.computerhistory.org/
  2. Science Museum (London). Mobile phone and messaging objects. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. BBC News. 'Merry Christmas: the first SMS text message.' https://www.bbc.com/news/technology-20555620

6.21Social Media Platforms

Key figure(s): Mark Zuckerberg (Facebook); Jack Dorsey (Twitter); Kevin Systrom (Instagram); multiple others

Social media platforms are websites and apps that let people publish to networks of friends or followers. They made the audience and the publisher the same population.

Period: 1997 (SixDegrees); 2004 (Facebook); 2006 (Twitter) | Key figure(s): Mark Zuckerberg (Facebook); Jack Dorsey (Twitter); Kevin Systrom (Instagram); multiple others

Key facts

  • Early sites included SixDegrees (1997), Friendster, MySpace, and others
  • Facebook (2004) and later Twitter, YouTube, Instagram, TikTok, and WeChat scaled the model
  • User-generated content is material made by ordinary users, not only by studios
  • Feeds are often ranked by algorithms that predict what you will click
  • The same tools organise both communities and conflict

What it is

A social media platform lets users create profiles, connect to others, and share text, images, or video with a network. The feed is a running list of updates, often sorted by an algorithm — a ranking rule — rather than only by time. User-generated content means the audience also produces the show. Picture a town square, a post office, and an advertising billboard fused into one app.

Who discovered or invented it

SixDegrees (1997) is often cited as an early social network. Friendster, MySpace, LinkedIn, Facebook (Mark Zuckerberg and colleagues, 2004), YouTube, Twitter, Instagram, TikTok, and many regional apps iterated the form. No one invented being social online; companies invented scalable, ad-funded architectures for it. Forums and blogs were ancestors. Some of those companies became among the largest on Earth.

When and how it emerged

Cheap digital cameras, broadband, and the smartphone created an endless supply of posts. Venture capital and a web used to free, ad-supported services fuelled growth. In the 2010s the phone camera became the default publisher. Regulation lagged behind. Campus directories became global public squares with private owners.

Why it mattered scientifically

These platforms turned social graphs — who knows whom — into data. They created new research in network science and new problems in content moderation. Attention became a measurable resource. The civic question is how ranking rules shape belief. A feed is not a neutral window onto the world.

How it shaped later developments

Live streaming, influencers, and short video are later formats. Messaging apps blurred into social media. Political campaigning, disaster mutual aid, and harassment all found the same buttons. Alternative and decentralised networks try to keep the useful parts with different ownership. The sequel is a struggle over rules and design.

How it changed human life

People gained ways to keep friendships across distance and to find rare communities. They also gained comparison, outrage, and coordinated falsehood. Journalism and teenage life were remade. Society is still learning how to handle speech at planetary scale. The same tool can gather people around knowledge — or scatter them with noise.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Our World in Data. 'The rise of social media.' https://ourworldindata.org/rise-of-social-media
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Social network.' https://www.britannica.com/topic/social-network
  3. Pew Research Center. Social media fact sheet. https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/fact-sheet/social-media/
  4. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy. 'Social Networking and Ethics.' https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/ethics-social-networking/

6.22Spaceflight

Key figure(s): Sergei Korolev (Soviet); Wernher von Braun (US); NASA; Yuri Gagarin; Neil Armstrong

Spaceflight is travel beyond Earth's atmosphere — satellites, crewed capsules, and probes to other worlds. It made Earth a place you can leave, and a place you can see whole.

Period: 1957 (Sputnik); 1961 (first human in space, Gagarin); 1969 (Moon landing) | Key figure(s): Sergei Korolev (Soviet); Wernher von Braun (US); NASA; Yuri Gagarin; Neil Armstrong

Key facts

  • Sputnik (1957) opened the space age; Yuri Gagarin orbited Earth on 12 April 1961
  • Apollo 11 landed Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin on the Moon in 1969
  • To stay in low Earth orbit, a craft must reach about 7.8 km/s — so fast it keeps falling around the planet
  • The International Space Station is a long-running crewed laboratory
  • Robotic probes have visited every planet in the solar system

What it is

Spaceflight means sending machines or people above the atmosphere into orbit or toward other worlds. A rocket carries its own oxygen so it can thrust where there is no air. In orbit, the craft falls around Earth so fast that the ground curves away beneath it — like tossing a ball so hard it never lands. Reaching the Moon or the planets needs still more energy. Most of the vehicle is fuel tank; the rest is careful engineering.

Who discovered or invented it

Konstantin Tsiolkovsky, Robert Goddard, and Hermann Oberth laid early theory and rocket experiments. Sergei Korolev's Soviet programme launched Sputnik and, in 1961, Yuri Gagarin. NASA, drawing on many teams and on Wernher von Braun's rockets, landed Apollo 11 in 1969. Valentina Tereshkova became the first woman in space in 1963; later astronauts and taikonauts from many nations widened the story. Robotic flight matters as much as crewed flight.

When and how it emerged

The space age began as Cold War competition mixed with genuine science during the International Geophysical Year. Missile technology, national prestige, and curiosity all pushed the calendar. After the Moon race came shuttles, space stations, and, in the twenty-first century, cheaper private rockets. Disasters — Apollo 1, Soyuz failures, Challenger, Columbia — showed how unforgiving the environment is.

Why it mattered scientifically

Spaceflight tested physics, life support, and systems engineering at the limit. It turned other worlds into places you can measure up close, and Earth into a planet you can study from outside. Materials, computing, and medicine gained useful spin-offs. The deeper gift is knowledge: planets are landscapes, not only points of light. Seeing Earth as a thin-wrapped sphere is both a scientific fact and a moral one.

How it shaped later developments

Communications and navigation satellites are the everyday harvest. Probes to Mars, the outer planets, and beyond the solar system extended the reach. China, India, Europe, Japan, and private firms are now regular actors. Return to the Moon and crews to Mars remain hard goals. The sky grew more crowded and more commercial.

How it changed human life

People gained weather forecasts, global communications, and shared images — Earthrise, a blue marble — that changed how many feel about the environment. Nations gained prestige and military vantage. A few hundred humans have seen Earth from orbit; billions have seen the photographs. Leaving the planet taught how rare and finite home is.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Spaceflight.' https://www.britannica.com/science/spaceflight
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Sputnik.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/Sputnik

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Smithsonian National Air and Space Museum. Human spaceflight and Apollo collections. https://airandspace.si.edu/

G. Space agencies, laboratories, and scientific organisations

  1. NASA. Human spaceflight history. https://www.nasa.gov/history/
  2. NASA. 60 Years and Counting (agency anniversary timeline). https://www.nasa.gov/specials/60counting/

6.23Telegraph

Key figure(s): Samuel Morse; Alfred Vail; William Cooke; Charles Wheatstone

The electric telegraph sent coded messages through wires in minutes. For the first time, information could travel much faster than a person, horse, train, or ship.

Period: 1837 (Cooke and Wheatstone, UK); 1844 (Morse's Baltimore-Washington line) | Key figure(s): Samuel Morse; Alfred Vail; William Cooke; Charles Wheatstone

Key facts

  • Cooke and Wheatstone installed an early commercial telegraph in Britain in 1837-39
  • Samuel Morse and Alfred Vail developed a practical American system and Morse code
  • The first lasting transatlantic cable was completed in 1866
  • Messages were tapped as dots and dashes, then decoded by clerks
  • News, markets, and empires began to run on near-instant information

What it is

A telegraph sends information as electrical signals through a wire. The signals stand for letters rather than carrying a person's voice. In Morse code, short and long signals form dots and dashes. A trained operator turns these patterns back into words.

Who discovered or invented it

Several people developed electric telegraphs. William Fothergill Cooke and Charles Wheatstone built an early railway system in Britain. Samuel Morse and Alfred Vail developed a practical American system and a widely used code. Carl Friedrich Gauss and Wilhelm Weber had shown an earlier electromagnetic telegraph, so credit belongs to more than one team.

When and how it emerged

Working systems appeared in the 1830s and 1840s. Railways needed fast signals, while newspapers and businesses wanted current information. Batteries, insulated wire, and networks of poles made expansion possible. After several failed attempts, a lasting transatlantic cable connected Europe and North America in 1866.

Why it mattered scientifically

The telegraph showed that information could be represented by simple electrical states and sent over a network. It also pushed engineers to understand resistance, signal loss, insulation, and long cables. Shared codes and operating rules were early network standards. They let equipment and people in different places work together.

How it shaped later developments

The telephone later sent voices over wires, and telex sent typed text. Digital networks still turn information into coded electrical or light signals. The telegraph also helped drive standard time zones because railways needed clocks and schedules to agree. In that sense, it acted like the first fast nervous system spread across continents.

How it changed human life

A family could receive urgent news on the same day instead of waiting for a letter. Prices, political decisions, and war reports moved much faster. Governments and empires could also control distant places more closely. The telegraph began the modern expectation that important news should arrive now.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Telegraph.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/telegraph
  2. Library of Congress. 'Samuel F. B. Morse Papers at the Library of Congress, 1793 to 1919.' https://www.loc.gov/collections/samuel-morse-papers-at-the-library-of-congress/about-this-collection/

H. Professional societies

  1. Engineering and Technology History Wiki. 'Telegraph.' https://ethw.org/Telegraph

6.24Telephone

Key figure(s): Alexander Graham Bell; Elisha Gray; Antonio Meucci

The telephone changes speech into an electrical signal and changes it back into sound at the other end. It made live conversation over long distances a practical service.

Period: 1876 (Bell's patent); widespread adoption from 1890s-1920s | Key figure(s): Alexander Graham Bell; Elisha Gray; Antonio Meucci

Key facts

  • Alexander Graham Bell was awarded a U.S. patent in 1876
  • Elisha Gray filed a related caveat the same day; Antonio Meucci had earlier work
  • Early systems needed human switchboard operators
  • Automatic exchanges and later digital networks scaled the idea
  • The phone made the human voice a thing that could travel wires

What it is

A telephone changes the vibrations of speech into a changing electrical signal. At the other end, a receiver turns that signal back into sound. The signal follows the shape of the voice, much as a groove on an old record follows the shape of music. Unlike the telegraph, the listener does not need to decode dots and dashes.

Who discovered or invented it

Alexander Graham Bell received a major U.S. telephone patent in 1876 and built a successful system. Elisha Gray filed notice of a related design on the same day, which led to a long priority dispute. Antonio Meucci had worked on voice communication earlier but did not secure a lasting U.S. patent. The telephone grew from several experiments, patents, and business decisions.

When and how it emerged

Public demonstrations and the first exchanges followed in the late 1870s. At an exchange, operators connected callers by placing plugs into a switchboard. Cities adopted service first because homes and businesses were close together. Rural service expanded more slowly as companies built longer lines.

Why it mattered scientifically

The telephone showed how a continuously changing signal, called an analogue signal, can carry speech. It brought acoustics, electricity, and later digital electronics together. Engineers had to solve switching, noise, long-distance signal loss, and reliable billing. These problems helped create modern communication engineering.

How it shaped later developments

Automatic exchanges reduced the need for operators. Undersea cables and satellites carried calls across oceans. Mobile networks removed the fixed wire to the user, and internet calling turned speech into digital data packets. The equipment changed, but the basic service remained live conversation at a distance.

How it changed human life

Families, doctors, newsrooms, and businesses could speak without travelling. Emergency calls could bring help quickly. The ringing phone also introduced a new kind of interruption and an expectation of being reachable. Switchboards created many jobs, especially for women, before automation removed much of that work.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Telephone.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/telephone
  2. Library of Congress. 'Alexander Graham Bell Family Papers at the Library of Congress, 1862 to 1939.' https://www.loc.gov/collections/alexander-graham-bell-papers/about-this-collection/
  3. Smithsonian Institution. 'The Telephone Turns 140.' https://www.si.edu/stories/telephone-turns-140

H. Professional societies

  1. Engineering and Technology History Wiki. 'Telephone.' https://ethw.org/Telephone

6.25Television

Key figure(s): John Logie Baird; Philo Farnsworth; Vladimir Zworykin; many broadcast engineers and standards bodies

Television sends moving pictures and sound to many receivers at once. It created a shared living-room culture of news, sport, and drama — later joined, not erased, by the internet.

Period: c. 1920s–1930s experimental broadcasts; mid-20th century mass household adoption | Key figure(s): John Logie Baird; Philo Farnsworth; Vladimir Zworykin; many broadcast engineers and standards bodies

Key facts

  • Mechanical and electronic scanning systems competed in the 1920s and 1930s
  • Philo Farnsworth and Vladimir Zworykin were among key figures in electronic television
  • Regular public broadcasts expanded before and after the Second World War
  • Colour standards and satellites extended reach and richness
  • TV became the dominant mass medium in many countries for decades

What it is

Television converts scenes into electrical signals, transmits them by radio waves or cable, and rebuilds moving images with sound on a distant screen. Early sets used cathode-ray tubes; later flat panels changed the furniture, not the idea of one-to-many live pictures. Schedules, channels, and later on-demand libraries organise what arrives. In plain speech, TV is the hearth that speaks in pictures to a whole nation at once.

Who discovered or invented it

Television emerged from many labs. John Logie Baird demonstrated mechanical scanning; Farnsworth and Zworykin advanced electronic cameras and tubes. Broadcast companies and national postal or telecom authorities set standards. Naming one inventor misleads; the medium is a stack of optics, electronics, and institutions.

When and how it emerged

Experimental broadcasts in the late 1920s and 1930s proved the concept; postwar prosperity put sets in living rooms across industrial countries. Colour, UHF, cable, and satellites thickened the offering through the late twentieth century. Digital compression and flat screens followed. Streaming apps now ride on internet pipes, still delivering the same social habit: watch together, apart.

Why it mattered scientifically

TV pushed cathode-ray physics, radio spectrum engineering, and the information theory of bandwidth. Synchronising lines and frames is precision timing. Colour encoding and later digital codecs are applied mathematics of perception. Studios became laboratories of lighting and signal integrity. Mass real-time video is an engineering triumph before it is entertainment.

How it shaped later developments

Advertising, politics, and sport reshaped themselves around broadcast slots. News cycles accelerated. The internet inherited attention habits trained by TV, even as it fragmented audiences. Closed-circuit and medical imaging share display ancestry. Without television's infrastructure of cameras and transmitters, tomorrow's video platforms would lack a template.

How it changed human life

Evenings reorganised around programme times; children grew up with shared characters. Distant wars and moon landings entered the home as moving images. Critics warned of passivity and propaganda; fans found community and joy. The glowing box — and now the glowing panel — remains how many people first meet the wider world each day.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Barnouw, Erik. Tube of Plenty: The Evolution of American Television. 2nd ed. New York: Oxford University Press, 1990.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Television.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/television-technology
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Philo Farnsworth.' https://www.britannica.com/biography/Philo-Farnsworth

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Television and broadcast technology stories. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Smithsonian Magazine. Features on the invention and cultural impact of television. https://www.smithsonianmag.com/innovation/how-television-invented-180963294/

6.26The Bicycle

Key figure(s): Karl Drais; Pierre and Ernest Michaux; John Kemp Starley; tyre and frame makers worldwide

The bicycle is a human-powered two-wheeler steered by handlebars and driven by pedals and usually a chain. It gave ordinary people cheap, fast personal mobility without a horse or engine.

Period: c. 1817 (draisine); 1860s pedals; 1880s safety bicycle with chain drive; mass adoption thereafter | Key figure(s): Karl Drais; Pierre and Ernest Michaux; John Kemp Starley; tyre and frame makers worldwide

Key facts

  • Karl Drais introduced a steerable two-wheeler pushed by the feet around 1817
  • Pedal-driven velocipedes and 'boneshakers' spread in the 1860s
  • John Kemp Starley's 1880s safety bicycle used equal-sized wheels and a chain drive
  • Pneumatic tyres and better roads made cycling comfortable for daily travel
  • Bicycles remain major transport in many cities and a foundation for later motorcycles and cars' two-wheel geometry

What it is

A bicycle is a steerable machine with two in-line wheels, a saddle, handlebars, and pedals that usually turn a chainwheel linked by chain to the rear wheel. The rider balances, steers, and supplies power; gears and brakes refine control. Frames of steel, aluminium, or composites hold the geometry that makes efficient rolling possible. In everyday language, it is personal transport powered by your own legs.

Who discovered or invented it

Karl Drais's laufmaschine opened the two-wheel idea. French makers such as the Michaux workshop added pedals to the front wheel. John Kemp Starley and others in Britain refined the chain-driven safety bicycle that became the modern template. Tyre inventors, frame builders, and club riders completed the system. No single patent owns cycling; a sequence of workshops does.

When and how it emerged

After 1817, hobby machines appeared in Europe. The 1860s boom made pedalling fashionable; high-wheel 'ordinaries' followed, then the safer diamond-frame design of the 1880s. Pneumatic tyres and paving turned bikes into commuting tools by the 1890s. Twentieth-century mass production put bicycles in factories' worker fleets and on developing-world streets. E-assist motors later extended range without erasing the human-power core.

Why it mattered scientifically

Cycling shows efficient conversion of muscle work into distance with low rolling resistance. It taught materials science in tubes, bearings, and spoke tension. Traffic engineering and public-health studies still measure bike share and fitness effects. Scientifically, the bicycle is a clean demonstration of leverage, balance, and human-powered energetics.

How it shaped later developments

Motorcycle and early automobile layouts borrowed bicycle shops' tubing, chains, and wheels. Women's dress reform and independent mobility campaigns rode with the safety bicycle. Urban planning now rediscovers bike lanes as climate and congestion tools. Component standards let global supply chains assemble frames and groups.

How it changed human life

Workers reached farther jobs; children gained unsupervised range; courtship and touring cultures flourished on two wheels. Cities densified around routes riders could climb daily. Injuries and road conflict arrived with speed. Even so, for billions the bike remains the cheapest door-to-door machine.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Herlihy, David V. Bicycle: The History. New Haven: Yale University Press, 2004.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Bicycle.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/bicycle
  2. Smithsonian Institution. Bicycle spotlight. https://www.si.edu/spotlight/bicycle

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Bicycle and transport collections. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories
  2. Smithsonian National Museum of American History. America on the Move. https://americanhistory.si.edu/america-on-the-move

6.27Transistor

Key figure(s): William Shockley; John Bardeen; Walter Brattain

A transistor is a tiny semiconductor device that switches or strengthens an electrical signal. It replaced hot, fragile vacuum tubes and made portable electronics and modern computers possible.

Period: 1947 (invention at Bell Labs); widespread commercial use from mid-1950s | Key figure(s): William Shockley; John Bardeen; Walter Brattain

Key facts

  • John Bardeen, Walter Brattain, and William Shockley created the first transistor at Bell Labs
  • The point-contact transistor worked in December 1947
  • A semiconductor is a material whose ability to conduct can be tightly controlled
  • Transistors are the building blocks of computer chips
  • The 1956 Nobel Prize in Physics recognised the invention

What it is

A transistor uses a small electrical signal to control a larger current. It can work as a switch or as an amplifier, which makes a weak signal stronger. Most transistors use silicon, a semiconductor whose electrical behaviour can be adjusted. Think of it as a very fast tap: a small control decides whether current flows.

Who discovered or invented it

John Bardeen and Walter Brattain demonstrated the first working point-contact transistor at Bell Labs in December 1947. William Shockley then developed the junction transistor, a design that was easier to manufacture. The three shared the 1956 Nobel Prize in Physics. Their work built on years of research into crystals and semiconductor surfaces.

When and how it emerged

Bell Labs wanted smaller and more reliable amplifiers and switches for telephone networks. Vacuum tubes did the job, but they used much power, produced heat, and often failed. Wartime research had improved knowledge of semiconductor materials. The new device moved into hearing aids, portable radios, and computers during the 1950s.

Why it mattered scientifically

The transistor showed that engineers could control quantum behaviour inside a crystal. It joined physics, chemistry, and electrical engineering in one useful component. Digital circuits use transistors as on-off switches to represent bits. Because each switch is small and reliable, engineers can combine enormous numbers of them.

How it shaped later developments

The integrated circuit placed many transistors and their connections on one piece of silicon. Continued shrinking later put billions on a single chip. Transistors now handle logic, memory, radio signals, sensors, and electrical power. Modern computing measures capability partly by how many useful transistors a chip contains.

How it changed human life

Transistors made radios small enough to carry and computers small enough to own. They later enabled mobile phones, medical devices, industrial controls, and the electronics inside cars. One transistor is simple, like one brick. Billions arranged carefully can form a powerful processor.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Transistor.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/transistor

D. Museum and archive resources

  1. Computer History Museum. 'The Silicon Engine: 1947 - Invention of the Point-Contact Transistor.' https://www.computerhistory.org/siliconengine/invention-of-the-point-contact-transistor/

G. Scientific organisations

  1. Nobel Prize. 'The Nobel Prize in Physics 1956.' https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/1956/summary/

6.28Washing Machine

Key figure(s): Multiple inventors of hand-crank and powered washers; later appliance firms that standardised automatic cycles

The washing machine uses mechanical agitation, water, and detergent to clean clothes with far less hand labour. Electric and automatic models turned laundry from a full-day chore into a push-button routine.

Period: c. late 18th–19th century mechanical washers; early 20th century electric machines; mid-century automatic cycles | Key figure(s): Multiple inventors of hand-crank and powered washers; later appliance firms that standardised automatic cycles

Key facts

  • Nineteenth-century wooden drum and agitator washers reduced beating clothes by hand on rocks or washboards
  • Electric motors in the early twentieth century powered wringers and drums in homes and laundries
  • Automatic machines combined wash, rinse, and spin cycles behind a sealed tub
  • Detergent chemistry and hot water systems worked with machines to lift soil more reliably
  • Shared laundromats and later home appliances redistributed domestic labour time across societies

What it is

A washing machine is an appliance that wets, agitates, rinses, and usually spins textiles to remove dirt. Early designs were hand-cranked tubs; later ones use electric motors, timers or controllers, and pumps. Detergent and temperature do chemical work while the drum does mechanical work. In everyday language, it is a box that scrubs the week's clothes so hands need not.

Who discovered or invented it

No single inventor owns laundry mechanisation. Nineteenth-century patents pile up for drums, dollies, and wringers across Europe and North America. Electric utilities and appliance companies later packaged motors with branded cabinets. Engineers of automatic washers refined seals, unbalance control, and cycle logic. The credit is collective: domestic need meeting workshop ingenuity.

When and how it emerged

Hand-powered washers appeared through the 1800s beside washboards and boiling copper. Electrification after about 1900 put motors in laundry rooms of wealthier homes and commercial laundries. Fully automatic machines spread widely after mid-twentieth century in industrial countries, then globally as incomes and grids grew. Front- and top-loaders competed on water use and fabric care. Microcontrollers later added sensors without changing the core wet-agitate-spin idea.

Why it mattered scientifically

Washers forced practical fluid dynamics, motor control, and materials that survive heat, alkali, and imbalance. Textile science measured shrinkage and colourfastness under machine stress. Time-use surveys made laundry labour visible as an economic quantity. Scientifically, the machine turned 'clean enough' into repeatable cycle parameters.

How it shaped later developments

Appliance industries, detergent marketing, and synthetic fabrics co-evolved with machine washing. Dishwashers and tumble dryers reused pumps, heaters, and sealed cabinets. Water and energy labels pushed efficiency engineering. Smart-home scheduling is a thin layer on the same drum.

How it changed human life

Hours once spent scrubbing returned to wage work, schooling, or rest — often unevenly by gender and class. Clean-clothing norms rose as machines made frequent washing cheap. Noise, microplastics from synthetics, and water use became new costs. Still, for many households the washer is the quiet engine of presentable daily life.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Cowan, Ruth Schwartz. More Work for Mother: The Ironies of Household Technology from the Open Hearth to the Microwave. New York: Basic Books, 1983.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Washing machine.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/washing-machine

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Smithsonian National Museum of American History. Collections on laundry and housework. https://americanhistory.si.edu/
  2. Science Museum (London). Domestic technology objects and stories. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

6.29World Wide Web

Key figure(s): Tim Berners-Lee; Robert Cailliau; Marc Andreessen (Mosaic browser)

The World Wide Web is a system of linked documents and applications viewed in a browser. Invented at CERN, it made the internet usable for ordinary reading, publishing, and sharing.

Period: 1989-1991 (proposal and first implementation); 1993 (Mosaic browser, public adoption) | Key figure(s): Tim Berners-Lee; Robert Cailliau; Marc Andreessen (Mosaic browser)

Key facts

  • Tim Berners-Lee proposed the web at CERN in 1989
  • The first website went online in 1991
  • HTML, HTTP, and URLs are the core trio: pages, request rules, and addresses
  • Berners-Lee released the technology royalty-free
  • Browsers such as Mosaic and Netscape made the web popular

What it is

The World Wide Web is a way of publishing and viewing information on the internet using hypertext: pages that contain links to other pages. A URL is an address; HTTP is the request-and-response rule; HTML is the language that describes a page. A browser is the program that fetches and displays it. Think of the internet as roads and the web as the shops and libraries built along them. Everyday speech often blurs the two, but they are not the same.

Who discovered or invented it

Tim Berners-Lee, a British computer scientist at CERN, the European physics laboratory, wrote the proposal in 1989 and, with colleagues including Robert Cailliau, built the first browser and server. The first site explained the project itself in 1991. He insisted the protocols stay open and free of royalties. Mosaic (1993) and then Netscape brought graphics and ease to a wide public. The inventor's choice to share was part of the invention.

When and how it emerged

CERN's physicists needed to share papers and phone lists across countries and different computers. The internet was mature enough, personal workstations were common, and open science culture helped. By the mid-1990s businesses rushed online. What began as a lab memo became a global medium in less than a decade.

Why it mattered scientifically

The web made a messy internet navigable with one idea: the link. It created a universal interface for databases, shops, and newspapers. The World Wide Web Consortium (W3C) tried to keep the language common. Accessibility, security, and the tension between documents and apps are design and scientific problems that followed. The web is both a library and a software platform.

How it shaped later developments

Search engines, social media, and cloud apps are built as websites or web-like apps. HTTPS added encryption for ordinary users. Mobile browsers put the web in a pocket. Some services then built walled gardens around it; the open web still sits underneath. Wikipedia and online journals show the original hope: shared knowledge.

How it changed human life

Ordinary people could publish without a printing press. Education, commerce, friendship, and news changed shape. So did advertising, tracking, and designs that keep people scrolling. Society gained the largest library ever built and a lasting struggle over who organises it. For many people the web is now the everyday hearth for learning and for noise.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'World Wide Web.' https://www.britannica.com/topic/World-Wide-Web
  2. CERN. 'The birth of the Web.' https://home.cern/science/computing/birth-web
  3. The first website (CERN historic copy). http://info.cern.ch/hypertext/WWW/TheProject.html

H. Professional societies and standards bodies

  1. World Wide Web Consortium (W3C). https://www.w3.org/

7. Emerging and Transformative Technologies

7.13D Printing (Additive Manufacturing)

Key figure(s): Chuck Hull (stereolithography); Carl Deckard (SLS); Scott Crump (FDM, Stratasys)

3D printing builds an object layer by layer from a digital model, adding material instead of cutting it away. It is a new way to make prototypes, spare parts, and some finished goods.

Period: 1984–1986 (Hull stereolithography); commercial use from 1990s; mainstream from 2010s | Key figure(s): Chuck Hull (stereolithography); Carl Deckard (SLS); Scott Crump (FDM, Stratasys)

Key facts

  • Chuck Hull patented stereolithography in 1986 and co-founded 3D Systems
  • Additive manufacturing is the industrial name: add material, do not subtract it
  • Methods include plastics, resins, metals, and even living cells in research
  • A digital file can become a physical part without dedicated moulds
  • Speed, strength, and cost still limit what is printed versus mass-produced

What it is

3D printing, or additive manufacturing, creates a solid object by stacking thin layers according to a computer model. Subtractive manufacturing carves a block; additive manufacturing draws the shape in material. Stereolithography uses light to harden liquid resin. Other printers melt plastic filament or fuse metal powder with lasers. The shared idea is: the file is the mould.

Who discovered or invented it

Chuck Hull invented stereolithography in the mid-1980s and helped commercialise it. Scott Crump developed fused deposition modelling (plastic filament) and co-founded Stratasys. Many later researchers added metal, ceramic, and bioprinting methods. The 2000s open-source RepRap movement put simple plastic printers in schools and garages. Invention here is a family of processes.

When and how it emerged

Industry needed faster prototypes than machining or mould-making allowed. Better computers, CAD software, and photopolymers set the stage. Patents shaped who could sell what until some expired and prices fell. The 2010s made 'a printer in every workshop' a slogan, only partly true. Aerospace and medicine adopted metal printing for complex, low-volume parts.

Why it mattered scientifically

Additive methods can make internal channels and lattices that are hard to machine. That is a new design freedom, not only a new machine. The field links materials science to software. Scientifically it asks how layer bonds, pores, and heat history determine strength. Quality control is the hard science, not the novelty of a printed toy.

How it shaped later developments

Hearing-aid shells, dental aligners, rocket injectors, and spare parts in remote places are real uses. Research prints tissue scaffolds and buildings, still mostly experimental. Certification questions follow: when may a printed part fly or go into a body? Mass production of simple objects still prefers moulds. 3D printing complements factories; it does not replace them.

How it changed human life

Designers gained overnight prototypes; some patients gained custom implants. Hobbyists gained a new craft and a pile of failed plastic. Society gained questions about guns, copyright, and local making. Production became a little more digital and a little more distributed. The printer on the bench is a small factory waiting for a file.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. '3D printing.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/3D-printing

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Rapid prototyping and additive manufacturing objects. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

H. Professional societies and standards bodies

  1. ASTM International. Additive manufacturing standards (ISO/ASTM 52900 family). https://www.astm.org/

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Smithsonian Magazine. 'A Brief History of 3D Printing.' https://www.smithsonianmag.com/innovation/a-history-of-3d-printing-180975475/

7.2Artificial Intelligence

Key figure(s): Alan Turing; John McCarthy; Marvin Minsky; Geoffrey Hinton; Yann LeCun; Yoshua Bengio

Artificial intelligence is the effort to make machines perform tasks that seem to need human intelligence, from recognising speech to playing games. Modern systems learn patterns from large amounts of data.

Period: 1950 (Turing's test); 1956 (Dartmouth Conference, AI coined); current boom from ~2012 | Key figure(s): Alan Turing; John McCarthy; Marvin Minsky; Geoffrey Hinton; Yann LeCun; Yoshua Bengio

Key facts

  • Alan Turing asked in 1950 whether machines can think, and proposed a test
  • The 1956 Dartmouth workshop popularised the name 'artificial intelligence'
  • Machine learning lets a program improve from examples rather than only from hand-written rules
  • Deep neural networks drove a boom after about 2012
  • AI is a set of methods, not a single conscious machine

What it is
