Artificial intelligence (AI) is a field of computer science that builds systems to classify images, translate language, recommend moves, or generate text and pictures. Machine learning is the dominant modern approach: the program adjusts internal numbers so that its outputs better match examples. A neural network is a layered calculation loosely inspired by brains, not a copy of a mind. AI today is powerful pattern machinery. It is not a person in a box.

Who discovered or invented it

Ada Lovelace and Turing sit at the philosophical start. The Dartmouth conference of 1956, organised by John McCarthy, Marvin Minsky, Nathaniel Rochester, and Claude Shannon, named the field. Early decades mixed logic, games, and later statistical learning. Geoffrey Hinton, Yann LeCun, Yoshua Bengio, and many others revived neural networks; a 2012 image-recognition result marked a public turning point. Large companies then scaled the methods with data and computing.

When and how it emerged

The idea is old; the current wave needed big data, GPUs (chips good at parallel maths), and the internet's labelled examples. Winters of disappointment came when promises outran hardware. The circumstances of the 2010s–2020s were cloud computing and vast text and image collections. Chatbots and image generators then made AI a household word. Hype and real capability arrived together.

Why it mattered scientifically

AI formalised questions about knowledge, vision, and language in testable ways. It also exposed limits: bias in data becomes bias in output; fluent text can be false. Scientifically it is both engineering and a mirror for cognitive science. Success in narrow tasks does not automatically yield general understanding. The field forces us to define what we mean by intelligence.

How it shaped later developments

Search, maps, medicine, translation, and science now use learning systems. Robotics and self-driving research depend on perception models. Regulation, safety research, and energy costs of training are the sequels. Older symbolic AI still matters where rules must be explicit. The later development is social: who is accountable when a model errs.

How it changed human life

People gained useful tools — better captions, faster drafts, medical image hints — and new risks of fraud, job disruption, and automated unfairness. Education and art are renegotiating originality. Society must decide where human judgement stays in the loop. AI helps when it is checked; it floods the world with confident error when it is not.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Russell, Stuart, and Peter Norvig. Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach. 4th ed. Pearson, 2020.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Our World in Data. 'Artificial Intelligence.' https://ourworldindata.org/artificial-intelligence
  2. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy. 'Artificial Intelligence.' https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/artificial-intelligence/
  3. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Artificial intelligence.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/artificial-intelligence
  4. Dartmouth College. Dartmouth Artificial Intelligence Conference historical notes. https://home.dartmouth.edu/about/artificial-intelligence-ai-coined-dartmouth

G. Space agencies, laboratories, and scientific organisations

  1. Google DeepMind. AlphaFold research overview. https://www.deepmind.com/research/highlighted-research/alphafold

7.3Battery Technology

Key figure(s): Alessandro Volta; John Goodenough, M. Stanley Whittingham, Akira Yoshino (Nobel Prize 2019)

A battery stores chemical energy and releases it as electric current. Lithium-ion cells, commercialised in the 1990s, made lightweight rechargeable power the norm for portables and, increasingly, cars.

Period: 1800 (Volta's pile); 1991 (Sony's first commercial lithium-ion battery) | Key figure(s): Alessandro Volta; John Goodenough, M. Stanley Whittingham, Akira Yoshino (Nobel Prize 2019)

Key facts

  • Alessandro Volta's pile (1800) was the first true battery
  • Gaston Planté invented the lead–acid battery in 1859 — still used in cars
  • Whittingham, Goodenough, and Yoshino developed lithium-ion chemistry
  • Sony commercialised the lithium-ion battery in 1991
  • Energy density — how much energy per kilogram — is the central race

What it is

A battery is a package of chemical reactions that push electrons through an external circuit. A cell has a positive electrode, a negative electrode, and an electrolyte — a medium ions can move through. Rechargeable batteries run the reactions in reverse when you apply current. Energy density is how much energy you store per kilogram or litre. That number decides whether a phone lasts a day or a car goes 400 kilometres.

Who discovered or invented it

Alessandro Volta announced the voltaic pile in 1800. Gaston Planté's lead–acid battery (1859) became the car starter battery. For portables, lithium-ion was the leap: Stanley Whittingham, John Goodenough, and Akira Yoshino made decisive chemical steps and shared a Nobel Prize in 2019. Sony sold the first commercial lithium-ion cells in 1991. Countless engineers then made them safer and cheaper.

When and how it emerged

Volta was answering Galvani's frog-leg mystery with a stack of metals and brine. The late twentieth century needed a battery that did not leak like earlier lithium metal cells. Consumer electronics and a materials-science search for hosts that can take lithium ions in and out drove the work. Safety incidents taught hard lessons about separators and control circuits.

Why it mattered scientifically

Batteries made electricity portable and schedulable. They are applied electrochemistry: voltage, capacity, and cycle life are measurable design goals. Grid storage now uses related cells to hold solar power after sunset. Scientifically the field is a race against physics and fire. Every percent of density is a research programme.

How it shaped later developments

Laptops, phones, cordless tools, and electric vehicles are lithium-ion stories. Research continues on solid-state, sodium-ion, and better recycling. Mining of lithium, nickel, and cobalt raises environmental and labour questions. The sequel is a circular industry, not only a better cell. Charging networks are the public face of the same chemistry.

How it changed human life

People gained untethered devices and, increasingly, quieter cars. Workplaces lost some cords and gained a new anxiety: battery health. Society must manage mines, fires, and e-waste. Portable power changed what 'away from a socket' means. Mobility and communication now assume a block of stored ions in the bag or the floor of the car.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Battery.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/battery-electronics
  2. Science History Institute. Battery history resources. https://www.sciencehistory.org/education/scientific-biographies/alessandro-volta/

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Voltaic piles and later cells. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

G. Space agencies, laboratories, and scientific organisations

  1. Nobel Prize. Chemistry 2019 (Goodenough, Whittingham, Yoshino) — lithium-ion batteries. https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/chemistry/2019/summary/
  2. International Energy Agency. Global EV Outlook (batteries and storage context). https://www.iea.org/reports/global-ev-outlook-2024

7.4Blockchain

Key figure(s): Satoshi Nakamoto (pseudonym); Vitalik Buterin (Ethereum)

A blockchain is a shared digital ledger whose pages (blocks) are chained with cryptography so that changing old records is extremely hard. It became famous as the backbone of Bitcoin and is now tested for other records.

Period: 2008 (Bitcoin whitepaper); 2015 (Ethereum smart contracts) | Key figure(s): Satoshi Nakamoto (pseudonym); Vitalik Buterin (Ethereum)

Key facts

  • Stuart Haber and W. Scott Stornetta published linked time-stamping in 1991
  • Satoshi Nakamoto's 2008 Bitcoin paper combined a chain with a way to agree without a bank
  • A hash is a digital fingerprint of data; change the data and the fingerprint changes
  • Energy use of some blockchains is a serious drawback
  • Not every database needs a blockchain; the tool fits some trust problems, not all

What it is

A blockchain is a list of records grouped into blocks, each block containing a cryptographic hash — a digital fingerprint — of the one before it. Altering an old block would break the chain of fingerprints. In public cryptocurrencies, many computers keep a copy and follow rules to agree on the next block (consensus). A ledger is simply a book of accounts. The novelty is a ledger that many strangers can share without a single clerk.

Who discovered or invented it

Haber and Stornetta's work on time-stamping documents is an important ancestor. In 2008 a person or group using the name Satoshi Nakamoto published Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System and launched the network in 2009. They combined a chain of hashes with incentives and a consensus rule (proof of work). Identity remains unknown. Later designers proposed other consensus methods and other uses.

When and how it emerged

The paper appeared after the 2008 financial crisis, when distrust of intermediaries was high, but the technical pieces were older. Widely available cryptography and peer-to-peer networks set the stage. Bitcoin proved that a decentralised digital money could run for years. Speculation, crime, and genuine experiment then arrived together. The idea outgrew the first coin.

Why it mattered scientifically

Blockchain made a public, checkable history of transactions. It is applied cryptography and distributed systems. It also showed the cost of some designs: proof of work uses large amounts of electricity. Scientifically the interesting claim is about trust: when can mathematics replace an institution? The honest answer is: sometimes, and not for free.

How it shaped later developments

Other cryptocurrencies, smart contracts (programs stored on a chain), and experimental registries for supply chains or certificates followed. Many projects failed or were unnecessary. Banks and governments tested wholesale digital money with different architectures. Regulation, energy, and a clearer sense of which problems need a chain are still being sorted.

How it changed human life

Some people gained a way to move value across borders without a traditional bank; others lost savings to fraud and volatility. Societies gained a debate about money, energy, and scams. A few artists and institutions used chains as a notary. Progress depends on using the ledger idea where it truly reduces harm, not where it only adds complexity.

Sources and references

B. Peer-reviewed articles and original scientific papers

  1. Nakamoto, Satoshi. 'Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System.' 2008. https://bitcoin.org/bitcoin.pdf
  2. Haber, Stuart, and W. Scott Stornetta. 'How to Time-Stamp a Digital Document.' Journal of Cryptology 3 (1991): 99–111.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Blockchain.' https://www.britannica.com/money/blockchain
  2. NIST. Blockchain technology overview (NISTIR 8202). https://csrc.nist.gov/publications/detail/nistir/8202/final

F. Online lectures and educational series

  1. MIT OpenCourseWare. 'Blockchain and Money' (Gary Gensler), 2018. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/15-s12-blockchain-and-money-fall-2018/

7.5Brain-Computer Interfaces

Key figure(s): Jacques Vidal and early BCI researchers; later clinical neural-engineering teams and implant developers

Brain-computer interfaces read or stimulate neural signals so people can control devices or regain sensory feedback without muscle movement. They link nervous systems to computers for research, medicine, and emerging consumer uses.

Period: c. 1970s laboratory demonstrations; clinical implants and noninvasive headsets thereafter; 21st-century neural prosthetics research | Key figure(s): Jacques Vidal and early BCI researchers; later clinical neural-engineering teams and implant developers

Key facts

  • Early laboratory work in the 1970s showed EEG patterns could drive simple computer cursors
  • Invasive electrode arrays can record from motor cortex to move robotic arms or cursors
  • Noninvasive headsets trade resolution for safer, easier setup
  • Clinical goals include restoring communication and movement after paralysis or amputation
  • Ethics of privacy, consent, and enhancement travel with every improvement in bandwidth

What it is

A brain-computer interface (BCI) measures brain activity — or delivers stimulation — and maps it to software or hardware actions. Sensors may sit on the scalp, under the skull, or inside cortex. Decoders translate spikes or rhythms into cursor moves, speech synthesizers, or robotic limbs; stimulators can feed touch or vision cues back. In everyday language, it is a bridge between thoughts-as-signals and machines.

Who discovered or invented it

Jacques Vidal popularised the BCI framing in the 1970s amid EEG control experiments. Generations of neuroscientists, neurosurgeons, and engineers refined electrodes, decoding algorithms, and clinical protocols. No single patent owns the bridge from brain to computer; it is a cumulative field. Companies later packaged research-grade systems for trials.

When and how it emerged

After academic demos, slow clinical translation occupied decades: monkeys and then humans controlling cursors and arms. Cochlear implants — related sensory prostheses — proved neural interfaces could be mainstream medicine. High-profile implant trials in the 2010s–2020s raised public attention and regulatory scrutiny. Consumer EEG toys remain limited beside medical research. Bandwidth and longevity of implants still gate daily independence.

Why it mattered scientifically

BCIs force real-time neuroscience, machine learning on noisy neural data, and biocompatible materials. They test theories of motor encoding and plasticity when the brain learns a decoder. Closed-loop stimulation links computation to therapy. Scientifically, they make mind–machine coupling an engineering variable.

How it shaped later developments

Neuroprosthetics, deep-brain stimulation refinements, and AR control schemes borrow BCI decoding ideas. Data standards for neural recordings grew with open science efforts. Military and gaming interest funds adjacent human–machine research with different ethics. Rehab robotics pairs exoskeletons with neural intent signals.

How it changed human life

A small number of patients regained typing or reach that paralysis had taken. Caregivers and clinicians navigate surgery risk versus autonomy. Public imagination swings between miracle and dystopia. Most people still interact with computers through hands and voice — but the frontier of access widened for those without them.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Wolpaw, Jonathan R., and Elizabeth Winter Wolpaw, eds. Brain-Computer Interfaces: Principles and Practice. Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2012.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Brain-computer interface.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/brain-computer-interface
  2. NIH. BRAIN Initiative. https://braininitiative.nih.gov/

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Neuroscience and medical technology objects. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Nature. Brain–machine interface subject page. https://www.nature.com/subjects/brain-machine-interface

7.6Carbon Capture

Key figure(s): Chemical engineers; energy companies; research labs developing capture solvents, membranes, and storage geology

Carbon capture separates carbon dioxide from industrial exhaust or directly from air so it can be stored underground or used. It is one toolkit among many for cutting net greenhouse-gas emissions.

Period: c. late 20th-century industrial separation know-how; 21st-century CCS/CCUS projects and direct-air capture pilots | Key figure(s): Chemical engineers; energy companies; research labs developing capture solvents, membranes, and storage geology

Key facts

  • Point-source capture strips CO₂ from power plants or industrial flue gases using solvents, sorbents, or membranes
  • Captured CO₂ may be injected into suitable geological formations for long-term storage
  • Direct air capture pulls dilute CO₂ from ambient air at higher energy cost per tonne
  • CCUS pathways reuse some CO₂ in industry while still requiring careful climate accounting
  • Cost, energy penalty, monitoring, and public acceptance shape how widely projects deploy

What it is

Carbon capture is a set of technologies that isolate carbon dioxide from gas streams — factory flues, hydrogen plants, or the open air — then compress it for storage or use. Capture units lean on chemistry and engineering: solvents, solid sorbents, membranes, or cryogenic steps. Storage typically means deep geological injection with monitoring. In everyday language, it is catching CO₂ before it joins the atmosphere's stock.

Who discovered or invented it

Industrial gas separation long predates climate policy; amine scrubbing and related methods came from chemical engineering practice. Climate-driven CCS assembled those tools with pipeline and reservoir expertise from oil and gas. Direct-air-capture inventors adapted loops to ultra-dilute CO₂. No single person owns the stack; it is applied chemistry plus geology.

When and how it emerged

Enhanced oil recovery used injected CO₂ for decades, teaching some storage lessons. Dedicated climate CCS pilots and a few commercial plants appeared from the 1990s–2010s onward with mixed economic results. Policy credits and carbon prices alter project viability year by year. Direct air capture moved from prototypes to early commercial tonnes in the 2020s at still-high cost. Scale required for climate targets remains far larger than today's fleet.

Why it mattered scientifically

Capture chemistry optimises energy per mole of CO₂ under dirty flue conditions. Reservoir science models plume migration, mineralisation, and leakage risk. Life-cycle analysis decides whether a project truly lowers net emissions. Scientifically, carbon capture turns the atmosphere's waste gas into a managed industrial flow.

How it shaped later developments

Hydrogen 'blue' pathways, cement and steel decarbonisation plans, and some bioenergy schemes assume capture. Monitoring, reporting, and verification standards grew for underground storage. Critics push harder on renewables and efficiency when capture is used to prolong fossil assets. Still, hard-to-abate sectors keep CCS on the roadmap.

How it changed human life

Most people meet carbon capture only in news about plants, pipelines, and climate pledges. Host communities weigh jobs against local risk narratives. If scaled wisely, capture can cut industrial emissions; if used as delay, it can slow other transitions. The technology's social meaning is still being negotiated.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. IPCC. Special Report on Carbon Dioxide Capture and Storage (2005) and later assessment chapters on CCS and carbon dioxide removal. https://www.ipcc.ch/report/carbon-dioxide-capture-and-storage/

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. IEA. Carbon capture, utilisation and storage. https://www.iea.org/energy-system/carbon-capture-utilisation-and-storage
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Carbon sequestration.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/carbon-sequestration

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Climate and energy technology objects and stories. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Global CCS Institute. Status reports and project database. https://www.globalccsinstitute.com/

7.7Cellular Agriculture

Key figure(s): Tissue engineers; food-tech researchers; startups and university labs growing animal cells for food

Cellular agriculture grows animal products from cells rather than raising and slaughtering whole animals. Cultured meat and related foods aim to decouple protein from traditional livestock footprints.

Period: c. early 21st-century tissue-engineering proofs; 2010s cultured meat demos; ongoing scale-up and regulation | Key figure(s): Tissue engineers; food-tech researchers; startups and university labs growing animal cells for food

Key facts

  • Tissue-engineering methods adapted bioreactors and scaffolds to grow muscle and fat cells for food
  • Public cultured-meat tastings and pilot approvals appeared in the 2010s–2020s in a few jurisdictions
  • Growth media, cell lines, and sterile scale-up dominate cost and engineering challenges
  • Related products include cell-based dairy proteins and other animal-derived molecules made without herds
  • Regulation, labelling, and energy use remain active debates beside technical progress

What it is

Cellular agriculture produces agricultural goods — especially meat, fat, or dairy proteins — by culturing animal cells or microbial hosts instead of farming whole animals for every kilogram. Cells multiply in bioreactors with nutrients, sometimes on scaffolds that shape texture. The goal is familiar foods with different supply chains. In everyday language, it is meat grown like a crop of cells.

Who discovered or invented it

The field inherits decades of medical tissue engineering and fermentation know-how. Academic and startup teams then targeted food rather than implants. No single inventor owns cultured meat; it is a convergence of cell biology, bioprocess engineering, and food science. Chefs and regulators joined late as products neared plates.

When and how it emerged

Proof-of-concept burgers and nuggets in the 2010s proved sensory possibility at high cost. Pilot plants and limited restaurant sales followed in places with novel-food pathways. Scale-up battles focus on cheap media, contamination control, and texture that matches whole cuts. Parallel microbial fermentation for animal proteins advanced on a related track. Mass-market status is still unfinished business.

Why it mattered scientifically

Cellular agriculture stresses sterile bioprocessing at food volumes and quantitative sensory science. It measures land, water, and greenhouse trade-offs against livestock baselines — with contested life-cycle analyses. Stem-cell and immortalised line biology become food-process variables. Scientifically, it asks whether animal tissue can be a manufactured material.

How it shaped later developments

Alternative-protein investment spilled into plant-based and precision-fermentation sectors. Bioreactor vendors found new customers beyond pharma. Culinary R&D reinvented marinades and fats for cultured blends. Policy fights over the word 'meat' foreshadow labelling wars elsewhere.

How it changed human life

For most eaters the plate has not yet changed; for researchers and investors it already has. Livestock communities watch for economic threat or niche coexistence. Ethical debates split among animal welfare, 'naturalness,' and energy justice. If costs fall, city protein supply could partially delink from pasture — with new industrial dependencies.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Good Food Institute. Cultivated meat and fermentation explainers. https://gfi.org/cultivated-meat/
  2. FAO. Food systems and livestock publications. https://www.fao.org/animal-production/en

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Science Museum (London). Food and biotechnology objects and stories. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. Nature. Cultured meat subject page. https://www.nature.com/subjects/cultured-meat
  2. FDA. Human food made with cultured animal cells. https://www.fda.gov/food/food-ingredients-packaging/human-food-made-cultured-animal-cells

7.8Electric Vehicles

Key figure(s): Charles Jeantaud (France, 1881); GM EV1 (1996); Elon Musk / Martin Eberhard (Tesla, 2003)

An electric vehicle uses motors powered by onboard batteries (or another electric source) instead of burning fuel in cylinders. After a false dawn a century ago, EVs returned as batteries improved.

Period: 1880s (early EVs); 1990s (GM EV1); 2008–present (Tesla and the modern EV era) | Key figure(s): Charles Jeantaud (France, 1881); GM EV1 (1996); Elon Musk / Martin Eberhard (Tesla, 2003)

Key facts

  • Electric cars were common in the 1890s–1910s before cheap petrol cars won
  • A motor turns current into rotation with high efficiency
  • Modern revival depends on lithium-ion packs and power electronics
  • EVs shift emissions from the tailpipe to the power station — and to the mine
  • Charging time and range remain the practical constraints for many drivers

What it is

An electric vehicle (EV) is a car, bus, or other vehicle driven by electric motors. In a battery EV, a pack of cells stores the energy; a controller feeds the motors. There is no petrol combustion in the drive cycle. Efficiency is high: more of the stored energy becomes motion than in a typical heat engine. The vehicle is, in effect, a computer and a battery on wheels.

Who discovered or invented it

Inventors in the late nineteenth century built capable electric carriages; for a time they outsold steam and petrol in some cities. Henry Ford's cheap petrol cars, and the starter motor that made cranking easy, pushed electrics aside. Modern pioneers include company programmes in the 1990s, then Tesla and, later, almost every major automaker. The motor is old; the pack and the software are new.

When and how it emerged

The first EV age died for range, cost, and oil abundance. The second age began when lithium-ion became cheap enough and climate policy made tailpipes a target. Smartphone battery factories could be scaled to cars. China, Europe, and others used standards and incentives. Charging plugs became a political object.

Why it mattered scientifically

EVs apply motor physics, battery chemistry, and grid engineering at once. They force honest accounting of life-cycle emissions. Regenerative braking — turning motion back into stored charge — is everyday physics in the brake pedal. The scientific story is systems integration. A car became an electrical appliance.

How it shaped later developments

Hybrids, electric buses, and e-bikes widen the family. Vehicle-to-grid experiments treat parked cars as storage. The sequel depends on cleaner electricity and better minerals. Hydrogen fuel-cell vehicles are a parallel bet for some heavy uses. Roads, tax, and repair shops are all being rewritten.

How it changed human life

Drivers gain quiet, strong acceleration and home charging — if they have a home charger. Cities gain less street-level exhaust; they may not gain less congestion. Workers in engine plants face transition. Society is trying to keep personal mobility while changing its energy source. The EV is a social project disguised as a drivetrain.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Electric automobile.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/electric-automobile
  2. U.S. Department of Energy. Alternative Fuels Data Center — electric vehicles. https://afdc.energy.gov/vehicles/electric
  3. Our World in Data. Electric car sales. https://ourworldindata.org/electric-car-sales

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. The Henry Ford. Early electric cars and the modern revival. https://www.thehenryford.org/

G. Space agencies, laboratories, and scientific organisations

  1. International Energy Agency. Global EV Outlook 2024. https://www.iea.org/reports/global-ev-outlook-2024

7.9Gene Therapy

Key figure(s): Multiple molecular biologists and clinician-scientists; vector engineers; later CRISPR-related therapy developers

Gene therapy treats disease by adding, repairing, or silencing genes inside a patient's cells. Viral vectors and newer editing tools aim at root genetic causes rather than only symptoms.

Period: c. 1970s–1980s lab groundwork; 1990s first clinical trials; 21st-century approved therapies and genome-editing tools | Key figure(s): Multiple molecular biologists and clinician-scientists; vector engineers; later CRISPR-related therapy developers

Key facts

  • Researchers proposed delivering working genes to compensate for defective ones decades before routine clinic use
  • Early trials in the 1990s proved concept but also revealed immune and safety risks
  • Viral vectors such as AAV became common delivery vehicles for therapeutic DNA
  • Some inherited blood, eye, and metabolic disorders now have approved gene therapies in certain countries
  • Genome editing (including CRISPR-based approaches) extends therapy from gene addition toward precise repair

What it is

Gene therapy is medicine that changes genetic material in living cells to treat or prevent disease. A vector — often a modified virus — may carry a healthy gene copy, or editors may cut and rewrite DNA or RNA. Treatment can target cells outside the body then return them, or deliver cargo inside the body. In everyday language, it tries to fix the instruction book, not only the symptoms.

Who discovered or invented it

No single inventor owns gene therapy. Molecular cloning, viral vector design, and clinical immunology each contributed. Pioneering clinician-scientists ran risky early trials that taught dosing and immune response. Later teams commercialised specific products for rare diseases. CRISPR pioneers expanded the editing toolkit that therapy developers now adapt.

When and how it emerged

After recombinant DNA tools matured, conceptual papers and animal work filled the 1970s–80s. Human trials in the 1990s brought both hope and setbacks, including serious adverse events that tightened oversight. The 2010s–2020s saw regulatory approvals for selected indications and booming investment. Cost, manufacturing, and long-term follow-up remain gates to wider use. Editing therapies are entering clinics on a careful timetable.

Why it mattered scientifically

Gene therapy is applied genomics, virology, and immunology in one package. It forces measurement of delivery efficiency, off-target edits, and durability of expression. Rare-disease genetics gained treatment hypotheses once delivery improved. Scientifically, it tests whether rewriting DNA can become routine pharmacology.

How it shaped later developments

Cell therapy, mRNA platforms, and gene-editing diagnostics share delivery and safety lessons. Manufacturing of viral vectors became its own industry. Ethics boards and access debates shape who receives very expensive one-time doses. Veterinary and agricultural gene tools travel adjacent paths with different rules.

How it changed human life

A few patients with once-untreatable genetic diseases gained functional sight, clotting factors, or immune repair. Families navigate uncertainty, trial eligibility, and cost. Public fear of 'playing God' sits beside demand for cures. Most common diseases are not yet gene-therapy problems — but the horizon moved.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

7.10Nuclear Fusion

Key figure(s): Plasma physicists worldwide; tokamak and laser-fusion programmes (e.g. ITER partners; national labs)

Nuclear fusion joins light nuclei so that mass converts to energy, the same process that powers stars. Engineers chase controlled fusion as a low-carbon, high-density energy source still being proven at power-plant scale.

Period: c. mid-20th-century research reactors and weapons physics; decades of magnetic and inertial confinement experiments; ongoing power-plant pursuit | Key figure(s): Plasma physicists worldwide; tokamak and laser-fusion programmes (e.g. ITER partners; national labs)

Key facts

  • Fusion powers the Sun by combining hydrogen isotopes under extreme temperature and pressure
  • Magnetic confinement (tokamaks, stellarators) and inertial confinement (lasers) are leading laboratory approaches
  • Net energy gain in brief experimental shots has been reported in some facilities, while continuous commercial power remains unachieved
  • Fuel cycles often focus on deuterium–tritium with challenges in materials and tritium breeding
  • International projects such as ITER aim to demonstrate sustained burning plasmas for research

What it is

Nuclear fusion is the merging of light atomic nuclei into heavier ones, releasing energy when the products weigh slightly less than the inputs. On Earth, researchers heat and confine plasma — ionised gas — until collisions fuse isotopes such as deuterium and tritium. Magnetic fields or implosion drivers hold the fuel long enough to react. In everyday language, it is trying to bottle a piece of the Sun safely.

Who discovered or invented it

Astrophysicists explained stellar fusion; weapons programmes and civilian labs then pursued terrestrial control. Generations of plasma physicists, engineers, and international consortia built tokamaks, lasers, and diagnostics. No lone inventor will 'finish' fusion; it is a long scientific-industrial campaign. Credit is institutional and collective.

When and how it emerged

Post-war research established confinement concepts; progress came in jumps of temperature, density, and confinement time. Large machines of the late twentieth and early twenty-first centuries approached break-even metrics without yet delivering grid electricity. Private ventures added compact designs beside megaprojects. Materials that survive neutron flux and heat remain pacing items. The calendar is still 'not yet commercial,' measured in decades of iteration.

Why it mattered scientifically

Fusion research drove plasma physics, superconducting magnets, high-power lasers, and radiation-resistant materials. It links nuclear physics to engineering at extreme conditions. Diagnostics of turbulent plasma advanced computational science. Scientifically, fusion is a laboratory for stellar processes and for energy denser than chemical fuels.

How it shaped later developments

Spin-offs include medical accelerators, advanced magnets, and plasma processing. Climate policy treats fusion as a possible late-century firm-power option beside fission and renewables. Workforce training in cryogenics and vacuum systems feeds other industries. Investment cycles teach patience and hype management.

How it changed human life

Daily life is not yet powered by fusion plants; the change is cultural and scientific: a shared long bet on clean baseload. Communities near labs see jobs and debate risk. If commercial fusion arrives, grids and geopolitics of fuel could shift; if delayed, other low-carbon tools must carry the load. Hope itself became an energy-policy variable.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Freidberg, Jeffrey P. Plasma Physics and Fusion Energy. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 2007.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Nuclear fusion.' https://www.britannica.com/science/nuclear-fusion
  2. ITER Organization. Project overview and fusion explainers. https://www.iter.org/
  3. IAEA. Fusion energy. https://www.iaea.org/topics/energy/fusion

  1. Science Museum (London). Energy and nuclear science objects and stories. https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories

7.11Quantum Computing

Key figure(s): Richard Feynman; David Deutsch; Peter Shor; experimental lab and company teams

Quantum computing uses quantum states such as superposition and entanglement to process information in ways classical bits cannot. It is still emerging, but already reshaping cryptography and materials research agendas.

Period: c. 1980s theoretical proposals; 1990s algorithms; 21st-century experimental processors and error-correction research | Key figure(s): Richard Feynman; David Deutsch; Peter Shor; experimental lab and company teams

Key facts

  • Feynman and others argued in the 1980s that quantum systems might simulate nature better than classical computers
  • David Deutsch formalised a universal quantum computer model
  • Peter Shor's 1994 algorithm showed factoring could be exponentially faster on a suitable quantum machine
  • Hardware approaches include superconducting circuits, trapped ions, photonics, and more
  • Error correction and scaling remain central engineering challenges before broad practical advantage

What it is

A quantum computer stores information in qubits that can exist in superpositions of 0 and 1 and become entangled with one another. Carefully chosen operations (gates) evolve those states so that measurement yields useful answers for certain problems. It is not a faster laptop for every task; it is a different computational resource. In everyday language, it harnesses quantum weirdness as a calculating tool — useful for some puzzles, ordinary for others.

Who discovered or invented it

Theoretical seeds came from physicists such as Richard Feynman and David Deutsch. Algorithm designers including Peter Shor and Lov Grover showed dramatic potential speedups. Experimentalists then built few-qubit devices and ever larger noisy processors. No lone inventor owns the field; it is a multi-decade research community.

When and how it emerged

Ideas crystallised in the 1980s; algorithms of the 1990s set targets. Laboratory qubits multiplied in the 2000s–2020s under national and corporate programmes. Claims of 'quantum advantage' for specialised tasks appeared alongside scepticism about error rates. Post-quantum cryptography began migrating standards in anticipation. The timeline is still open-ended research, not finished consumer rollout.

Why it mattered scientifically

Quantum computing binds computer science to quantum information theory and experimental physics. It forces precision control of decoherence, noise, and entanglement. Chemistry and materials simulation are flagship scientific use cases. Cryptography must rethink hardness assumptions if large machines arrive. The field turns fragile quantum behaviour into an engineering variable.

How it shaped later developments

Cloud access to small quantum processors trains a new workforce. Semiconductor and cryogenics supply chains adapt to qubit needs. Governments fund quantum networks and sensing as siblings of computing. Software stacks invent hybrid classical–quantum workflows.

How it changed human life

For most people the change is still indirect: news headlines, research budgets, and future-proofed encryption. Specialists already redesign algorithms and security roadmaps. Hype and disappointment cycles affect investment and education. If scalable machines succeed, drug design and optimisation could shift; if not, the scientific tools built along the way still remain.

Sources and references

A. Books and major scholarly works

  1. Nielsen, Michael A., and Isaac L. Chuang. Quantum Computation and Quantum Information. 10th anniversary ed. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 2010.

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Quantum computer.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/quantum-computer
  2. NIST. Quantum information science programmes. https://www.nist.gov/quantum-information-science

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. Computer History Museum. Computing history resources. https://www.computerhistory.org/

J. Press, popular science, and general reference

  1. IBM. What is quantum computing? https://www.ibm.com/topics/quantum-computing

7.12Renewable Energy Technologies

Key figure(s): Bell Labs (silicon solar cell, 1954); Darrieus and other wind turbine pioneers; multiple researchers

Renewable energy technologies gather power from flows that nature replenishes — sunlight, wind, water, Earth's heat. They are old ideas meeting modern materials, now central to cutting carbon emissions.

Period: 1954 (first practical silicon solar cell); 1970s–present (wind, solar, storage development) | Key figure(s): Bell Labs (silicon solar cell, 1954); Darrieus and other wind turbine pioneers; multiple researchers

Key facts

  • Water wheels and windmills are ancient; electric hydro dams are nineteenth-century
  • The silicon photovoltaic cell was demonstrated at Bell Labs in 1954
  • Modern wind turbines grew from the 1970s–80s and then fell sharply in cost
  • Renewables do not run out on human timescales, but they vary with weather
  • Grids need storage and transmission to use them at large scale

What it is

Renewable energy means useful power from sources that refill themselves: sunlight, wind, falling water, waves, and geothermal heat. A photovoltaic cell turns light into electric current; a wind turbine turns moving air into rotation and then into electricity. These sources produce little carbon dioxide while running, unlike coal or gas plants. They are not always 'on' — the sun sets; the wind drops — so a system needs backup or storage.

Who discovered or invented it

Water and wind machines are ancient. Electric hydropower dates to the 1880s. Edmond Becquerel saw the photovoltaic effect in 1839; Bell Labs' Daryl Chapin, Calvin Fuller, and Gerald Pearson made a practical silicon cell in 1954. Space programmes used solar first when cost did not matter. Danish and other engineers revived large wind turbines after the 1970s oil shocks. No single inventor owns renewables.

When and how it emerged

Climate science and cleaner-air laws gave the modern push; falling silicon and turbine costs made the economics real in the 2010s. Public research, subsidies, and large-scale manufacturing mattered. Hydro has geographic limits and ecological costs. The twenty-first century is the first time solar and wind are cheap enough to reshape whole grids.

Why it mattered scientifically

These technologies apply semiconductor physics, aerodynamics, and power electronics. They force a new kind of grid science: forecasting weather as forecasting supply. Life-cycle analysis — counting the energy to make a panel or a turbine — is part of honest accounting. The scientific importance is that energy can be harvested from flows, not only from stocks of fossil carbon.

How it shaped later developments

Batteries, hydrogen, and long-distance transmission are companion developments. Heat pumps pair well with clean electricity. Geothermal and better hydro design continue. The hard problem is integration: how to keep lights on on a still, dark evening. Politics of land use and mineral supply are now part of energy engineering.

How it changed human life

Communities can gain local power and cleaner air; they can also fight over ridgelines and deserts. Households with panels gain a new relationship to the weather. Society is attempting a planned shift of its energy metabolism. Progress here means keeping services while cutting the carbon that destabilises climate. Renewables are tools for that bargain, not a complete story by themselves.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Our World in Data. 'Renewable energy.' https://ourworldindata.org/renewable-energy
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Renewable energy.' https://www.britannica.com/science/renewable-energy
  3. Science History Institute. Silicon solar cell, Bell Labs, 1954. https://www.sciencehistory.org/stories/magazine/solar-power/

G. Space agencies, laboratories, and scientific organisations

  1. International Energy Agency. World Energy Outlook. https://www.iea.org/reports/world-energy-outlook-2024
  2. IRENA. Renewable energy statistics and technology briefs. https://www.irena.org/

7.13Robotics

Key figure(s): George Devol (Unimate, 1961); Joseph Engelberger; Boston Dynamics; Rodney Brooks; Masahiro Mori

Robotics is the design of machines that sense, decide, and act in the physical world. From factory arms to surgical helpers, robots extend what a programmed body can do.

Period: 1961 (Unimate, first industrial robot); 1970s automated manufacturing; 21st century AI-enabled robotics | Key figure(s): George Devol (Unimate, 1961); Joseph Engelberger; Boston Dynamics; Rodney Brooks; Masahiro Mori

Key facts

  • The word 'robot' comes from Karel Čapek's 1920 play R.U.R.
  • Unimate, installed in 1961 at a General Motors plant, was an early industrial robot
  • A robot typically combines sensors, a computer, and actuators (motors)
  • Most working robots are specialised, not human-shaped
  • AI is increasingly the 'decide' part of sense–decide–act

What it is

A robot is a machine that can sense its surroundings, compute a response, and move or manipulate objects. Actuators are the motors and pistons that do the moving. Most industrial robots are arms in cages, not walking people. Autonomy is a scale: some robots repeat a fixed path; others adapt. Robotics is the engineering of useful action, not the myth of a metal servant.

Who discovered or invented it

Karel Čapek's play gave the world the word (from a Slavic root for forced work). George Devol and Joseph Engelberger commercialised Unimate, which stacked hot metal in 1961. Earlier automata and Grey Walter's tortoises were ancestors. Japan, Germany, and others then built a robot industry. Today's surgical assistants and warehouse bots have many parents.

When and how it emerged

Factories after 1960 wanted repeatable, dangerous jobs done without fatigue. Computing, hydraulics, and later cheap sensors made that possible. The 1980s boom and bust taught that robots need the right task, not only a clever arm. Twenty-first-century warehouses and hospitals created new markets. Humanoid demos attract cameras; specialised machines earn the money.

Why it mattered scientifically

Robotics is a meeting of mechanics, control theory, and computer vision. It forces precise definitions of safety around moving mass. Scientifically it tests how much intelligence is needed for a simple physical act — grasping a cup is still hard. The field also feeds back into AI: a robot that fails in the real world cannot hide in a dataset.

How it shaped later developments

Welding, painting, chip handling, and now last-mile delivery experiments are sequels. Drones are flying robots. Soft robots and exoskeletons try to work nearer humans. Regulation and labour questions follow: who is liable, and who is displaced. Home robots remain limited because homes are messy.

How it changed human life

Workers gained relief from some toxic and repetitive tasks and lost some bargaining power where automation was used only to cut headcount. Consumers gained cheaper goods and, sometimes, worse service. Society must decide how to share productivity. Robotics keeps reshaping the dignity and the danger of work.

Sources and references

C. Institutional and encyclopedic sources

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Robot.' https://www.britannica.com/technology/robot-technology
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica. 'Karel Čapek.' https://www.britannica.com/biography/Karel-Capek

D. Museum, archive, and library resources

  1. The Henry Ford. Unimate and industrial robots. https://www.thehenryford.org/collections-and-research/digital-collections/artifact/157068/

H. Professional societies and standards bodies

  1. IEEE Robotics and Automation Society. https://www.ieee-ras.org/
